# One isolated publication reconstruction

Read the execution plan and entire allowed reader, including every shared block, in bounded untruncated output. Confirm the sole absolute destination and report complete reading; wait for authenticated controller release before authoring. Only locked source, inventory, resolved judgments and calculated scores are assessment evidence. Reference metrics benchmark exposition only. Do not browse, inspect other debates, peer outputs, sessions, environment or credentials, use paid services or spawn agents. Use the exact model and effort in the plan.

Identity, move selection, sections, positions, source ownership, timecodes, ratings and all scores are frozen. Author only summary, quotes, moves, overall, logicalExtension, noveltyMap and editorialReview. The checker assembles these parts with the frozen skeleton. Never author new score fields or recalculate results.

## Current source and scope

Motion: Do human beings have free will?
Pro: Daniel Dennett. Con: Robert Sapolsky.

Assess only the two formal opening speeches and two reply rounds, approximately 2:24–42:43, including Sapolsky’s short direct answer and Dennett’s continuation within the final reply round. No separate formal closing speeches occur. The subsequent moderator-led discussion, audience Q&A, final moderator question and replies, poll results, introduction and logistics are excluded. Neither speaker receives credit, penalty, inferred concession or failure-to-reply inference from excluded material.

The motion is Do human beings have free will? Dennett explicitly accepts determinism and argues for an evolved, socially supported capacity for reasons-responsive self-control and responsibility. Do not assign him an uncaused or libertarian will, universal equal capacities, or ultimate responsibility for becoming a responsible agent. He acknowledges luck, diminished capacity and exceptions.

Sapolsky denies free will while explicitly accepting ordinary intentions, choices and biological self-control. He argues that their causal origins are outside the agent’s control. He says genes determine next to nothing in isolation and emphasizes gene-environment interactions across development and evolutionary history. Do not recast his position as simple genetic determinism, denial that choices occur, or denial that reasons can causally change people.

Both speakers accept instrumental punishment and reward; neither is committed here to gratuitous suffering or indiscriminate release of dangerous people. Sapolsky’s qualification that less pain and more happiness are good is explicitly restored after Dennett quotes his machine statement. Dennett acknowledges the addendum. Preserve that correction; do not portray Sapolsky as finally denying that anything good can happen to humans.

Sapolsky quotes Dennett’s runner analogy and comments about a two-stage model. Dennett responds that the quoted passages continued with qualifications or rejection, and distinguishes meeting an intuition from endorsing a model. These are source-reported textual disputes, not independent verification of either book. Record the actual live quotation, challenge and reply; do not import outside book passages or assume any speaker’s allegation establishes accuracy.

Dennett distinguishes determinism from predictability, invokes deterministic chaos and unconstrained generation of variation, and denies that evolution proves indeterminism. His coin-flip wording shifts between randomness and controllability. Sapolsky accepts option generation while maintaining that the resulting chooser and values have unchosen causal origins. Preserve these distinctions without making a penalty out of recognition noise.

Automatic captions contain occasional mangled technical words, mixed interjections and a garbled final punishment sentence. The final reply still clearly recommends a fair and non-awful sanction system. Do not quote or penalize the recognition-corrupted phrase about the punished person. Reported statistics, medical examples and book quotations remain the speakers’ evidence, not independently verified scientific or textual facts.

Five targeted automated audio checks confirm the opening disagreement, the machine-quotation qualification, Dennett’s quotation-context replies and the formal-round boundary. They are supplementary text, not replacement canonical captions or direct human listening. Any selected passage with materially uncertain ownership or wording remains below high confidence and requires its own prescribed verification.

Full caption coverage includes all later excluded discussion. That availability does not make excluded answers, the money/mortgage analogy, later edge-case debate, audience-study claims or final poll eligible. The sole inter-event gap above two seconds is 2.36 seconds at the moderator’s handoff inside the formal reply round. There is no evidence of an omitted substantive formal speech.

Featured quotations must be exact complete 3–18 word thoughts with clear referents and all material qualifications. Do not use an incomplete quote from an opponent’s reported words as the speaker’s own final view, and do not use a concession cut before its immediate challenge.


## Exact parts schema and limits

- summary: 8–35 words, normally 18–28, accurately describing the locked comparison.
- quotes: {pro:{text,context},con:{text,context}}. Each text is an exact 3–18-word substring of one same-side quoteEligibleExactSpans entry; target 6–14 words. Preserve capitalization and punctuation, and choose clear uncorrupted words. Context is 12–55 words explaining the actual inferential setting. The quotation must express a COMPLETE THOUGHT when read without that context; no unfinished object, premise or conclusion and no omitted meaning-changing condition. Context may clarify a referent but must never complete a fragment. Inspect adjacent source sentences; choose another eligible quote rather than invent completion words. Third-party reported claims must not be presented as the speaker’s own view.
- moves: exactly {moveId,words,critique} for every inventory move in inventory order. Words is 8–55 words; target 22–28 and explain the claim, its offered reason or comparison, and the intended inference without adding premises. Overall mean must be at least the minimum recorded in reference-metrics.json and at most one quarter may be below 20. A source-required short item needs a specific editorial explanation.
- Every critique is 105–130 words and at least 880 characters. Exactly four sentences, in this order, beginning “Strongest feature:”, “Principal limitation:”, “Live burden:”, “Locked score:”. Each ends with terminal punctuation. The last starts “Locked score: N ”, copying N from the corresponding skeleton card. Prefer 120–126 substantive words when needed for the character minimum; do not pad. Avoid sentence-breaking abbreviations.
- overall: {pro:{strengths:[strings],blunders:[{text,links:[]}]},con:{...}}. At least three concrete strengths and two material blunders per side. Each item at least eight words, usually 15–30. Keep links empty for the later blind tag review.
- logicalExtension: {pro:{finalArgument:{thesis,premises,conclusion},newArguments:[{title,text}]},con:{...}}. Thesis at least 12 words; 4–6 premises each at least 12 words; conclusion at least 15 words. Each side has 2–4 genuinely new reinforcing arguments, titles 2–8 words and text 45–130 words. Natural depth near the reference corpus is preferable to barely meeting minima: roughly 25–40 words for thesis/conclusion, 18–30 per premise, 75–110 per reinforcing text, where reasoning warrants it.
- noveltyMap: one record for each side's finalArgument and every newArguments item: {side,field,path,hypothetical:true,sourceMoveIds,explanation}. Paths use dot notation such as candidate.logicalExtension.pro.newArguments.0. Use current move IDs that motivate the refinement. Explain in at least 18 words exactly what the new reasoning adds or repairs relative to those moves; never recycle a novelty sentence.
- editorialReview: {completeSourceCompared:true,allCardStrengthsAndLimitationsReviewed:true,correctBurdenPreserved:true,nonformulaicAiProseReviewed:true,ordinaryPunctuationPreserved:true,noJudgmentOrScoreChanges:true}. Assert each only after actually doing it.


## Editorial obligations before first save

Compose each description and four-sentence critique independently from its own source and resolved judgment. No sentence banks, padding or interchangeable score-band conclusions. Identify an actual strength, material limitation and remaining burden consistent with the fixed value. Preserve both sides’ genuine responses and later qualifications; excluded Q&A and recognition noise cannot become speaker defects. Reported scientific, historical and theological claims remain speaker claims, not externally verified facts.

The site discloses AI Contribution once. Write direct philosophical reasoning with distinct inferential additions and meaningful novelty explanations. Compare beginnings, argument progression and conclusions across both sides; reject interchangeable model-would framing and mirrored sentence scaffolds. Use ordinary punctuation. Strengthen both sides against their actual strongest objections without claiming invulnerability or attributing added reasoning to speakers. Tags and blunder links remain empty for later blind review.

DOCUMENT docs/assessment-production/standalone-debates-v1/debate-300/source/assessment-source-notes.md
# Frozen source restrictions

Assess only the two formal opening speeches and two reply rounds, approximately 2:24–42:43, including Sapolsky’s short direct answer and Dennett’s continuation within the final reply round. No separate formal closing speeches occur. The subsequent moderator-led discussion, audience Q&A, final moderator question and replies, poll results, introduction and logistics are excluded. Neither speaker receives credit, penalty, inferred concession or failure-to-reply inference from excluded material.

The motion is Do human beings have free will? Dennett explicitly accepts determinism and argues for an evolved, socially supported capacity for reasons-responsive self-control and responsibility. Do not assign him an uncaused or libertarian will, universal equal capacities, or ultimate responsibility for becoming a responsible agent. He acknowledges luck, diminished capacity and exceptions.

Sapolsky denies free will while explicitly accepting ordinary intentions, choices and biological self-control. He argues that their causal origins are outside the agent’s control. He says genes determine next to nothing in isolation and emphasizes gene-environment interactions across development and evolutionary history. Do not recast his position as simple genetic determinism, denial that choices occur, or denial that reasons can causally change people.

Both speakers accept instrumental punishment and reward; neither is committed here to gratuitous suffering or indiscriminate release of dangerous people. Sapolsky’s qualification that less pain and more happiness are good is explicitly restored after Dennett quotes his machine statement. Dennett acknowledges the addendum. Preserve that correction; do not portray Sapolsky as finally denying that anything good can happen to humans.

Sapolsky quotes Dennett’s runner analogy and comments about a two-stage model. Dennett responds that the quoted passages continued with qualifications or rejection, and distinguishes meeting an intuition from endorsing a model. These are source-reported textual disputes, not independent verification of either book. Record the actual live quotation, challenge and reply; do not import outside book passages or assume any speaker’s allegation establishes accuracy.

Dennett distinguishes determinism from predictability, invokes deterministic chaos and unconstrained generation of variation, and denies that evolution proves indeterminism. His coin-flip wording shifts between randomness and controllability. Sapolsky accepts option generation while maintaining that the resulting chooser and values have unchosen causal origins. Preserve these distinctions without making a penalty out of recognition noise.

Automatic captions contain occasional mangled technical words, mixed interjections and a garbled final punishment sentence. The final reply still clearly recommends a fair and non-awful sanction system. Do not quote or penalize the recognition-corrupted phrase about the punished person. Reported statistics, medical examples and book quotations remain the speakers’ evidence, not independently verified scientific or textual facts.

Five targeted automated audio checks confirm the opening disagreement, the machine-quotation qualification, Dennett’s quotation-context replies and the formal-round boundary. They are supplementary text, not replacement canonical captions or direct human listening. Any selected passage with materially uncertain ownership or wording remains below high confidence and requires its own prescribed verification.

Full caption coverage includes all later excluded discussion. That availability does not make excluded answers, the money/mortgage analogy, later edge-case debate, audience-study claims or final poll eligible. The sole inter-event gap above two seconds is 2.36 seconds at the moderator’s handoff inside the formal reply round. There is no evidence of an omitted substantive formal speech.

Featured quotations must be exact complete 3–18 word thoughts with clear referents and all material qualifications. Do not use an incomplete quote from an opponent’s reported words as the speaker’s own final view, and do not use a concession cut before its immediate challenge.

### core-con

of why luck does not even out over time, as Dan has often said. Instead, bad luck amplifies and good luck and privilege amplifies in the opposite direction. What we also see is a realm where these are not just biological influences. If you're talking about genes, you're talking about their evolution. You're talking about the epigenetics of your fetal life that regulate them. You're talking about the proteins they made 20 minutes ago. It forms one continuous arc of biology in which there is not a crack therein into which you can shoehorn free will. If it was 500 years ago and somebody said, wow, we just noticed when people get hammered over the head, their behavior changes. The brain may be relevant. That's one thing. We know a ton now. We know enough to present the challenge. Show me a brain, a person who has just done something completely independent of every one of those biological, environmental influences I just outlined, and you've proven there's free will. And at this point, we know enough of this biology that the onus is on the free will believers. Unless you can show evidence of people's actions, people's behavior, people's personalities, proclivities, desires, et cetera, being independent of their biological and environmental history, you've presented no evidence that free will exists. And the only default stance there is, is to doubt that it exists, to not accept its existence.

Over and over and over, our intuitions have proven wrong. Over and over, when we realize our intuitions are very poor litmus test and we recognize we are intuitively punishing and rewarding and judging and all of that for things people had no control over. Over and over, when we recognize that these intuitions, in fact, are based on responsibility, which is simply not there, the world becomes a better place. It really is a very good thing that we figured out witches don't control the weather and we figured out that rotten psychoanalytically poisonous mothers don't cause schizophrenia and that people with a certain variant of a gene for the leptin receptor have trouble having their brain satiate rather than them just hating themselves and thus eating themselves into obesity. At every one of these steps, our initial intuitions have proven very, very suspect and wrong. And at each step, when we actually learn what's going on, we we subtract responsibility out of it. And not only doesn't the societal roof fall in, it becomes a much better place to live in.
